ParlaMint-SI_2011-06-16-SDZ5-Redna-29.ana.u179	Thank you. Mr Kolarič has the floor to prepare Mr Kek.
ParlaMint-SI_2011-06-16-SDZ5-Redna-29.ana.u180	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Madam Secretary, colleagues, colleagues! Most of the debaters today speak mainly of electoral units, constituencies, and I will discuss a few other matters that I think are still important in this law, and I think it is right to think a little about them. In particular, as regards the composition of the electoral committees and the voting by post, both from Slovenia and abroad. If I go to the first topic, the composition of the electoral committees, which is in the basic text of the law in Article 41 and the amendments in Article 11, govern the way in which the electoral committees appointed by the District Electoral Commission are drawn up. In practice, however, there are often complications where applicants, particularly political parties, local communities and others, have, due to relatively short deadlines, proposed certain candidates for leadership or membership in these electoral committees, without actually verifying the interest or ability of the proposed candidates to carry out their duties on the electoral committee in the event of appointment. This leads to a relatively large number of cancellations of proposed and appointed members for work. The proposed amendment of the law in this Article, with the exception of the obligation of the appointed members to the electoral committee to notify the competent district electoral commission as soon as possible that the work on the electoral committee on the day of the vote will not be able to perform, does not address the problems mentioned. In view of the above, however, it would be advisable to consider additional additions to this Article, which would go cumulatively in both directions. That is, the extension of the deadline for candidates to propose candidates to work on the electoral committee and the mandatory mutual consent of candidates or candidates to work on the electoral committee. In this way, the electoral boards would be given greater confidence or guarantee for their full-time operation. The second issue in the work of the electoral committees and, consequently, the problem with the composition of the electoral committees, which has not been addressed by the proposer at all, is the issue of resolving problems where, in the opinion of the proposer, the electoral committee is not made up in accordance with the law. In particular, these are cases where the competent district electoral commission does not at all implement or correctly implement the provisions of the law which provide for the order in which the proposals of individual candidates are taken into account, in particular in the case of political parties. Thus, the compositions of the electoral committees are "de facto" illegal, as, on the one hand, candidate proposals for candidates with a priority right to propose are not taken into account. On the other hand, as a consequence, the proposals of candidates which comply with the provisions of the law should not be made up of electoral committees. In such cases, the said article of the law should provide for an appropriate legal remedy, such as an objection to the constituency committee against the composition of the electoral committee, with the legal interest, in particular by law, of a certain candidate for membership in the electoral committees. In practice, some such problems have already arisen, but the objections raised have been rejected, mainly on grounds that there is no legal basis for such objections. While the latter is true, it is not possible to overlook Article 25 of the Constitution, which guarantees the right to legal protection. In this case, however, the right of applicants is not legally protected and, from that point of view, this legal void should be properly regulated. This means primaryly ensuring the possibility of opposition, complaints and secondary appropriate judicial protection, for example in an administrative dispute. This is a problem that has not been fully addressed by the proposer, and others seem even more important to me, namely by post. The novelty of the law foresees an important novelty, i.e. the general possibility of voting by post in the Republic of Slovenia as well. The Law on Elections in the National Assembly was essentially based on the fact that it represents a vote by post, both at home and abroad, a way of voting, which is among the exceptions and is applied only in specific cases. The current regulation thus foresees that in Slovenia, by post, caregivers may vote at home for the elderly who do not have a permanent residence in the home, and for the electorate who are in hospital treatment if such voting methods are applied to the competent electoral commission in a timely manner. Citizens of the Republic of Slovenia who have an electoral right, whether permanent or temporary abroad, may vote by post, but it is essential to point out that both of these categories of citizens are in a very different legal position, which can be controversial from a constitutional point of view. In fact, citizens of the Republic of Slovenia who reside abroad on a permanent basis are considered to have been allowed to vote automatically in the post office by means of the amendment of the Law on Elections to the National Assembly in 2007 if they have decided to do so. They may also have chosen to vote at their diplomatic-consular representation. In doing so, they do not need to apply to the competent district election commission for post voting, as is the case with the category of nationals of the Republic of Slovenia who do not have a permanent residence abroad. Prior to the mentioned amendment to the 2007 Act, this was the obligation of all citizens of the Republic of Slovenia who had the right to vote, whether resident permanently or temporarily abroad. Thus, the Automatism of sending all electoral material, electoral cards, ballots, envelopes to over 50,000 titles of citizens of the Republic of Slovenia who are permanently residing abroad and who have an electoral right has several shortcomings, and therefore, in the previous term, efforts were made by Members to change such arrangements, but the majority of them did not want to listen. The shortcomings which the current proposed amendment does not address are, in particular, the following: the high costs of sending such quantities of electoral material by post abroad, the abuse of the possibility of double voting. The fact is that this category of electoral beneficiaries is considered to be able to decide for themselves whether to vote by post or by diplomatic-consular representation or even by both. Thirdly, it's the inequality of the electorate. It was pointed out that this is primarily about unequal treatment of non-resident voters and those who have a temporary residence abroad. Moreover, in practice there are differences in the category of voters who have permanent residence abroad. The fact is that some voters are still sending requests for voting by post, which means they are monitoring electoral developments and are active in the process of exercising their electoral rights. As a result, these voters are not able to change their minds and vote at their diplomatic-consular representation, since the electoral directory already lists them as voting by post. However, if these activities were not previously initiated, they could at any time be reconsidered and voted in the diplomatic-consular representation. This means that the legal regime penalises the voter's activity and, on the other hand, rewards his passivity, which is, in my opinion, a kind of absurdity. However, as the fourth option I mentioned, there is also an opportunity to abuse the right to vote in person and in secret. It is a fundamental flaw in the conduct of elections by post. Everyone has the right to decide for himself whether or not to participate in the election and if he or she attends, he or she has the right to decide how to vote. In this regard, however, the problem arises firstly that there is no possibility of more effective influence on the electorate, of exercising the right to vote and of exercising the will, not necessarily of its own - in this case there is no one, of having an electoral authority overseeing the exercise of the vote itself, and therefore the scope of possible abuses is significantly greater. The next problem in voting by post is linked to the voter's legitimacy. The postal way of sending electoral material does not in any way ensure that: (a) the electorate - the electoral beneficiary receives the material personally, as the material is not heated in the principle of personal service as is known by the Law on Administrative Procedure. If we had such a regulated legal solution, we would, of course, have high postal costs. Secondly: that the electorate should vote in person because the voter's legitimacy in these cases replaces his signature on the electoral card, which is a significantly lower criterion for verifying the voter's identity than, for example, a ID card. Moreover, the electorate's signature cannot even be verified by the electoral authority, as it does not know its signature. The voter's position by post would, therefore, suggest at least a change in the way that the identity of his signature is confirmed either before an official or credible person or authorities in a foreign country. As already mentioned, the proposal for the amendment of the Law on Elections in the National Assembly provides for a general possibility of voting by post in the Republic of Slovenia as well. Such a decision represents a significant change in the electoral system and would therefore indicate a more thorough debate in the professional, political and other public. In view of the substantial emphasis on some of the solutions to the amendment of the law, which foresees the abolition of electoral thefts and the introduction of preferential voting, and so significantly interferes with the current electoral system, some of the other important issues of the proposed novelty remain undetected. One of these issues is certainly, as I said, the introduction of post elections, which are probably the first step towards the later possible introduction of electronic elections. However, it is important to ask what the general introduction of the post elections entails. The good part of this introduction is certainly to bring the elections closer to the polls and voters and, as a result, to increase electoral participation. It is true, however, that in this solution we have been following countries such as Germany, where they have had such a system for years, and Austria, which introduced it in 2007. Surely, the positive experiences of these countries can lead to some sort of regulation. Nevertheless, it is necessary to ask and answer the question of what is the fundamental meaning of the current regime, which provides for the conduct of elections by post only as an exception. The fundamental substantive basis of the answer to this question is to ensure the right of each voter to vote in person and to ensure his or her confidentiality. The classic vote in the electorate, the actual voter's arrival, his proper legitimacy with the electoral body and the voter's decision to vote in the election booth, which ensures the secrecy of his decision, is a generally accepted standard for the conduct of elections which makes it possible to do so effectively. As already mentioned, post voting is much more exposed to various possible abuses, which are certainly not only theoretically negligible, but unfortunately can also be very real. In order for abuses or at least attempts of abuse not to take place, the drafter of the amendment also indirectly recognises when, in Article 65, he provides that it is prohibited to take photographs or otherwise take pictures of a voice. In practice, therefore, the applicant has found that the scope of such problems is possible and personally I do not think that it has solved it definitively. Introducing, I would say, electors A and B. On the contrary, voter A must come alone to vote, activate himself in order to exercise his electoral right, identify himself before an electoral body with a personal document, sign before an electoral body in an electoral directory, vote in an electoral box, and be exposed to the proper control of the body so that he can not possibly paint or record the ballots and thereby abuse his electoral right as evidence of how he voted and has received any counterservice or possible benefit. On the other hand, voter B is practically voting at home and at the national cost of sending mail with electoral material, not signing any electoral directory, but simply sending a request to the competent electoral authority that he wishes to vote by mail, which the electoral authority duly marks in the electoral directory, is legitimate only by signing the electoral card, and the electoral authority cannot verify the identity of the signature, since its signature is not known and is not subject to any control of the electoral authority. A simple comparison of the exercise of the same right of voters in one way or another shows that the proposed changes and additions to the law in this work unfortunately promote, as I said, the inequality between voter A and B and I think this is very controversial. In view of all of this, it is necessary to reconsider the consequences of the introduction of elections by post in Slovenia and abroad, and whether it would be reasonable not to carry out a more thorough public debate, not only on electoral districts, but on electoral units, but on these particularly important issues. Thank you very much.
